[[Note: The following table of contents is not the final text because rationalisation and editorial corrections are pending]] [[DESCRIPTION OF PRACTICE: According to Parliament's 15-11-2019 authorisation, the minutes of its NH's meeting of Friday 15 November 2019, with regard to the vote on the whole draft law: ‘Under the Convention of the Council of Europe signed at Maglingen/Macolin on 18 September 2014 on manipulation of sports games, urgent measures to combat violence in sport, conversion of the Hellenic Olympic Committee to NCI, ratification of this new statute and other provisions’.]] Ladies and gentlemen, we are entering the SPECIFIC FEDERATION Parliament's decisions: a debate on the revised provisions of the Constitution in accordance with Rules 110 of the Constitution and 119 of the Rules of Procedure. Under Rule 119 of Parliament's Rules of Procedure, the debate on the revised provisions is conducted in accordance with the ordinary legislative procedure, i.e. by applying the provisions of Rules 95 to 104 of the Rules of Procedure. The debate refers to the content of the revised provisions, as laid down in the decision of the IZ Plenary Session and as their contents have been formulated and proposed by the Review Committee. The Conference of Presidents on 7 November 2019 and 14 November 2019 decided to make available for the conclusion of the debate on the principle and on the individual themes a total of five meetings and a vote session, as provided for in the Constitution for the vote a meeting. In particular, with regard to the debate on the principle, I propose that the time limits to be allocated to the appropriate application of the provisions of Rule 97 of the Rules of Procedure, i.e. 15 minutes for the general rapporteurs and special speakers, 15 minutes for the parliamentary representatives, seven minutes for the registered speakers and twenty-five minutes for any minister who wishes to speak. The speakers will be registered with the electronic system, from the speech of the first rapporteur-general until the end of the speech of the second Secretary-General, namely the second speaker. The debate on the revised provisions I propose that four meetings be held as follows: At the first meeting - that is tomorrow, Tuesday - which corresponds to the first and second thematic section of the Constitutional Review Committee, namely Relations between Church and State, Religious Freedom, Oath and Individual and Social Rights, Tuesday, as I said, 19 November 2019, to discuss Articles 3(1) and 2, 3(3) (repealing paragraph), 3 (interpretation), 13(5), 33(2), 59(1) and 2, 5(2), 21(1) and 3, 21 (additional paragraph 7), 22(1), 2, 4 and 25(3) of the Constitution. At the second meeting, which corresponds to the third, fourth and eighth thematic section of the Constitutional Review Committee - i.e. institutional provisions, referendum, People's Legislative Initiative and Election of the President of the Republic - on Wednesday 20 November 2019, discuss Articles 37(2), 38(1) and (2), 68(2), 84(2), 28(2), 44(2), 73 and 32(4) and (5) of the Constitution. At the third meeting, which corresponds to the fifth and ninth section of the Constitutional Review Committee - i.e. Uninterrupted MPs, Prosecution against Members of the Government, Election System and Stealings of Election - on Thursday 21 November 2019, discuss Articles 62, 86(3), 86 (introduction of an interpretative statement) 54(1), 54 (insertion of an interpretative statement), 54 (insertion of an interpretation declaration) and 56 (insert paragraph 5) of the Constitution. At the fourth meeting, which corresponds to the sixth and seventh section of the Constitutional Review Committee - i.e. Military Judges, Independent Authorities, Regional Organisation of the State, OTA and Final and Transitional Provisions - on Friday 22 November 2019, discuss Articles 96(5), 101A, 101(1) and 4.1 (introduction of an interpretative declaration), 102(2), 112(4) (within the abolition of paragraph), 113 (repeal), 114(1), 114(2) (repealing paragraph), 115(1) and 2, 115(3) (repealing paragraph), 115(4) (in the same way as the removal of paragraph) and 119(1) (in the same way) of the Constitution. In the course of the debate, Mr President, I would like to thank the rapporteurs for their excellent report. Members of Parliament who fall within the respective section. In order to better organise the debate on the modules in terms of speaking time and in order to have more time at our disposal, I propose adding to the list of speakers that I have mentioned, in accordance with the relevant Rules of Procedure, up to two special rapporteurs from the New Democracy Parliamentary Group and up to two special rapporteurs from the SYRIZA Parliamentary Group. The speakers will be registered with the electronic system from the speech of the first general rapporteur until the end of the speech of the second special speaker, who is the second special speaker - do not take the second speaker to you - is the fourth speaker. Tuesday and Wednesday's meetings will begin at 9.30 p.m. On Thursday the meeting will begin at 12 noon, due to the celebration of the Armed Forces Day, on which the House has a capital involvement. Friday's meeting will start at 10:00. The list will also be drawn up for each individual meeting, again, at its beginning, regardless of the list of speakers at the previous meeting. Finally, on Monday, 25 November 2019, the meeting will begin at 10.00 with the speeches, exclusively, of the Presidents of the Parliamentary Groups and will be concluded with the vote on the revised provisions, at the same time, in accordance with the Constitution and Rules of the Parliament, as well as the adoption of the resolution of the current I Revision House. Indeed, ladies and gentlemen, it begins today and will last all week, a top parliamentary procedure, the process of Revision of the Constitution, which began during the previous House and is completed with the First Session of this House, in accordance with the provision of Article 110 of the Constitution. Revision of the Constitution is a parliamentary and political process. But, above all, it is a process of reproduction, at a more advanced level, of the constitutional word, which was delivered to writing, the captivity of writing, since 1975, continued in 1986, further in 2001 and 2008, so that the turn of this House could come today to relive the parliamentary speech, which was immobilised in the context and limits of the written text of the Constitution. That is why today's parliamentary speech is, in fact, nothing but an attempt to develop, correct and, possibly, to restore these shortcomings which life itself imposed during the historic time that has passed since the last Review to date. This is the charm of the fact that the orality of the parliamentary speech is the vitality given to a text, which, as Phaedros said to Plato, "has been delivered to scripture and died". So this process, which has always the need for a "father" to be able to function, rectify, guided, today enters its final phase. At this stage, then, we are being called with a vividness of an oral, rosy speech, which will be made on the contrary and, as we pointed out by the previous House, it will be conducted 'in advance and by debt', i.e. from conflicting, from different perceptions, which are based on different values, to be led to compositions. This is fate, value and purpose of liberal democracy. Why am I saying that? In the course of the Committee's work, which lasted a month and a half and worked out all the articles proposed for the revision of the Constitution, a great deal of effort was made to meet this treaty. And, indeed, we are all pleased that, according to the parliamentary speech, it has been formulated and expressed by the different party groups involved in this process and by all those who have taken the floor, the concern has been that there should be a quality, a high parliamentary reason. This has been achieved in many respects too. We must, however, bear in mind that this is a political process, and it is a process in which we are called upon, through the orality of the conflicts, to include the proposals for revision under the rule of Article 110. I am saying this because, during the debates, scientific opinions were made and there were little moments when we saw that the committee had, in fact, consumed in the controversy of scientific perceptions, suggestions, thoughts and opinions, which actually did nothing but carry back into this room the disputes. Many times it happened that parties' ideological views were turned into scientific assurances. This, however, does not satisfy the need for a revision of the Constitution, which - I repeat - is political, is not scientific. And in the sense of this policy, we have to say that it is done within the framework of a parliamentary procedure. It is in the context of a parliamentary procedure, but at times bad for Parliament itself. And that actually gives her a very dynamic one. Because this debate should resist a number of prejudices that have been created in civil society, about the deterioration of institutions from the economic crisis. The ten years of the economic crisis are now common belief that it has not only altered democratic processes, it has not led to the destruction of constitutional institutions, but also to a wall of distrust against the functioning of representative democracy. So a dialogue, which during the 1920s, had been strongly recorded, comes back at the forefront. And this dialogue begins exactly with the same suspicions. It starts with the betrayal of the representatives - what we said'representative betrayal' - and the parliamentary imperfections - namely that in Parliament - as many people have said, and, in the past years, this was the permanent incubation - there are three hundred who sit and do nothing, there are the three hundred who have delivered the country to the mercy of a evil crisis, against which they do not have the means to resist and create the will of the state needed to uphold its resurrection to the mighty of the earth. Here, ladies and gentlemen, you will allow me, in an incident, to recall the arguments that had then been exchanged between two top-level law, which are absolutely essential and will lead us to this debate as well. Then, in 1923, for the first time, Professor Carl Schmidt was published for parliament and democracy. There, in short, this judiciary said that parliamentarianism has been strayed from its purposes, has been removed from its founding myths and now Parliament has become the place where free and public debate is not the best argument, the maximum coercive, to convince the opponent. On the contrary, there are parties, which have become very great powers, which, in secret counseling, lead to some sort of solutions and decisions behind the people's backs. Just this reproach against parliamentaryism took over and effectively challenged another great Viennese professor of law, Hans Kelsen. So he left us a very important case, which today is the medicine against all the diseases presented by parliamentaryism and democracy, because of this treaty, which SYRIZA colleagues had called during the debate 'the post-democratic treaty'. So what saves democracy today is the same medicine that saved it, and then, of course, after that, the throat of chillerism, Nazism and fascism. So what we need to keep in mind is that democracy does not believe in absolute truths. Democracy and parliamentarianism are not intended to produce political truths and, above all, through the discussions in Parliament, we do not always come to the right solution. Because there are these imperfections of parliamentaryism, it does not mean that we should abandon parliamentaryism and democracy. Because parliamentarianism is democratically legitimised when the discussions within the National Representation are intended to bring together opposing views and lead to a compromise between different perceptions, but also divergent interests. That is the essence of liberal democracy. There will be no SYRIZA New Democracy, no New Democracy SYRIZA through the discussions that are taking place here. But these discussions should be conscious, aware that neither New Democracy can ignore SYRIZA nor SYRIZA can ignore the New Democracy. To God, I do not turn to a new climate of bipolarism, which I mention the two parties. But because, against this background, there are discussions between the majority and the officers, I am obliged, if you like, to make the message I want to give in my words clearer. That is exactly why I am talking, for example, about the two parties. At this stage, then, I think we have served precisely that perception, that democracy is an expression of political relativism. Democracy has no worthy foundation. Democracy does not empower or guide visions of cosmospherics. Democracy is the system of governance through codified procedures. In democracy the will of the state is formed and formed through an institutionalised conflict of understanding and interests. That is why compromise is the ideal of democracy. And in that context, I think we have moved. Of course, we could be braver, we could be more irresponsible toward the ideas of every party. However, it is certain that, if we had insisted, we would have achieved better choices through this procedure as well with regard to the revision of Article 16. Because I believe many of you, gentlemen, have inside you 'if we had joined the view of amending Article 16'. And if, in the decision we take next week, we also amended this article so that there would also be non-State, non-profit-making higher education institutions in Greece, Greece would have taken a step forward. The third and most serious point, in my opinion, is that we have also lost the opportunity to stand up to this routine of producing anti-constitutional laws from Parliament itself, through the introduction of a mechanism to prevent constitutionality by the Supreme Special Court. Indeed, I think you have shown an indifference, and you should not. However, it is true that we have succeeded in agreeing on seven major areas. And these areas cover the whole range and context of the Constitution. It is primarily the amendment of Article 21(1) which, for the first time and in the indication of the social solidarity that must be the principle of social rule of law, with a proposal from the New Democracy, is added and now there will be a system of financial protection for the family against poverty by introducing the minimum guaranteed income. The second case is Article 68(2), which, in fact, we have now clearly formulated - because the previous wording of Article 68 was unclear - the right of a minority to provoke two committees of inquiry during the parliamentary term. The establishment of a committee of inquiry is now being established as a means of parliamentary scrutiny, i.e. as a means of government control. This was the mission initiated by England and was essentially guaranteed by an order in the Baimar Constitution. It is said, in fact, that it was a proposal from Max Webber, who was then a Member of Parliament. The third issue, and very serious, is that we have limited the cases for which Parliament is willing to waive the immunity of Members for acts they do during their term of office. Because what really matters is that the Member should only be protected in those acts which are directly relevant to the exercise of his parliamentary duties. That is the essence of democracy, and that is the essence of protecting the representative of the people for every event in which they are defending their position. Article 86 was also a very large section, because we unanimously all Parliament's wings have decided that we should finally abolish the amortization deadline which has included the exercise of Parliament's procedural right to punish ministerial crimes in a suffocating manner. Now there is no longer any blocking of time for the House to exercise over many years, which basically coincides with the limitation of their respective crimes, the exercise of its right to punish those who have been delinquents or felonies in the performance of their duties and were State Secretarys or Ministers. There are two very serious provisions, however, which are also large areas. It is the provision of Article 54, with the addition of paragraph 4 on the vote of the relatives, who, for the first time in forty-five years, all parties have the great concern and we have the commitment to ensure that the vote of the non-residents is finally facilitated. This will indeed be a revolutionary intersection that we are going to do. And now the expression of Rule 62, which says that the Member is a representative of the nation, will be completed. It will be completed, as far as possible, because it will indeed have to be restricted to this very possibility. The great offer of this Review House is that we have finally finished with Article 32(4), which, if the President of the Republic fails to elect three successive, and by an increased majority, votes during the same parliamentary term led to the dissolution of the House. It is precisely this disconnection that is the great offer to democracy and our culture. Finally, of course, there is also the provision of Article 95(6), which is fully assimilated by the military judges, the judges of the Armed Forces, with the ordinary judges, and which is now leaving an entire era, which completely removed us from this memory of the military courts, which are traumatic, the experiences that exist for many of their operation. It is also very important that the revision of Article 101a, concerning the reduced majority of the members of the administration of the independent administrative authorities, should be elected. Ladies and gentlemen, I think what we have achieved is very important. Indeed, there were compromises and we made them with a very great democratic effort, with arguments which, on all sides, understood the parliamentary reason. What I want to say, however, is that, in the context of this debate that is taking place today, we should serve him to do that. Indeed, in the context of this relativeisation of the values and perceptions and interests represented by the groups, which are today as parties in Parliament, should give the people a balanced new constitution. In conclusion, I would like to say and remind you that all this effort being made by exchanging arguments, all this effort being made by meditation on the chances of being accepted, on the possibilities opened for the Revision of the Constitution is not a trivial process. It is not a process that falls short of active political action, vita activa. It is a spectacular action, it is a spectacular reflection of serious problems that define our lives and, above all, of the principle of autonomy, the principle of self-determination, which is satisfied when citizens can and obey rules which they themselves, through their representatives, have established. This is the majesty of democracy, which is far away from every city that publishes miracles or believes in doctrines. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-18-S1-commons.u10	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the debate on the Revision of the Constitution is at the highest moment in the city. It is not politically or ideologically neutral. Why are we coming to do this right now? What is the function of Revision of the Constitution? It regulates social and political conflicts that have been mediated at the level of the real constitution. In other words, it reflects the developments of political time in the constitutional flaw. If so, what was the major political event of the previous period? Undoubtedly, the crisis. It is true that the Constitution for the Crisis is not responsible, although there are extreme neoliberal voices that give it responsibility, because it was not sufficiently market-friendly, because it is too social. However, contrary to these voices, the Constitution has been one of the victims of the crisis. Why? Due to the Memorandum of Understanding, a 'parallel' has been applied for the second time in the constitutional history of our country, a set of rules which have been in conflict with the fundamental provisions of the Constitution and at the level of democracy and parliamentaryism and especially at the level of social rights, such as derogations from the parliamentary procedure, laws with an article, unelected Prime Ministers, and in particular this major impact on the conquests of the social state in collective bargaining and social rights. That is why our review proposal has had a double aim: on the one hand, to strengthen democracy, to protect freedoms and rights more effectively, as we tried at the legislative level during our governance, with important areas at the level of equality - I remind you of the cohabitation pact, I remind you of the important changes in the right to diversity at many levels, I remind you of the reforms in terms of the Code of Nationality - but, at the same time, these efforts to shield the Constitution with new guarantees so that in the future we can avoid a new 'parliament'. That is why we have proposed substantial arrangements to guarantee social conquests at work, in health, in the protection of the common, but also in the institutional, procedural counterweights. Not only the introduction of institutions of direct democracy, although they were central to our proposals, but also proposals that strengthened the representativeness of the culture. Always be a Member of the Prime Minister. Do not treat the parliamentary function. Above all, our important proposals for the change of Article 86 and the interpretative declaration on the protection of the price of the political world. And the New Democracy has reacted to the crisis. It also proposed provisions so that the Constitution would, obviously, be adapted in a completely different, opposite ideological and political direction from us. He proposed the constitutionalisation of neoliberalism, by means of the so-called 'golden rule', a debate that has now been abandoned by everyone in Europe, namely the adoption of the rule of balanced budgets, which would undermine any possibility of protecting the welfare of the social state in crisis and any developmentist impulse. These were the proposals for the revision of Article 106. We have in the previous House, the nomination parliament, the prevalence of similar proposals, which would substantially alter the social character of our Constitution, which defines our culture, not just as a formal rule of law, but as a social rule of law. On the other hand, the electoral prevalence of the New Democracy means that the whole is not going to be adopted, but only a part of the positive proposals we have put forward, from this coherent grid of dozens of proposals, which - unlike some aspects of the Chamber, especially the KINAL - have claimed that they were not concerned with the ordinary citizen - they would be very important to him because they would protect wages, health and social protection. Only those provisions that could not afford political costs will be passed on to New Democracy not to support them. This does not mean, however, that the debate before us does not have a political impact. Because it highlights our deep ideological and political differences, which are important for the development of the real constitution. I should like to highlight two points above all in relation to these very important differences. The first was the attempt to work on the constitutional review by the government majority. And the second thing I want to point out is that it can be seen from the positions put forward by the New Democracy in the Review that its ideological backwardness to positions that are not just behind the 2014 Review proposal, but in many cases they are behind the discussions themselves in the 1975 Constitutional Parliament. The concept of regularity and extreme political hypocrisy in the review process has begun, you will remember it, with Mr Mitsotakis' proposal to vote on all parties which will be revised in the first House, to raise a hundred and eighty votes in the proposed Parliament and then, by a simple majority, the next to regulate the content of these provisions. Why didn't anyone in the past have such a plausible idea? Because it is contrary to Article 110 of the Constitution. Article 110 seeks consent, consents on the substance of the Review, that is, it does not want the political forces to coincide only with what provisions will be revised, but also with how they will be revised. That is why the caselaw of both the Supreme Special Court and the Council of State stipulates that the direction of the proposed parliament is binding on it, but cannot alter the direction they have. That is why Mr Mitsotakis' original proposal was not just politically misleading, but also unconstitutional. If consensus were to be ensured by offsetting opposite and incompatible positions, similar agreements would have been in the past when the government majorities and the Capacity opposition did not separate ideologically abysmal as they are today. Sadly, however, with the same perception of playing and not of institutional seriousness, the New Democracy has also moved to the proposed Parliament, with, for example, the question of how the President of the Republic was elected. While there was a general agreement not to interrupt the life of the parliamentary term, New Democracy was removed from the proposal originally supported under the 2014 Review, which was similar to the current SYRIZA proposal. That's where you'd say you'd rethinked it. What, though, happened in the nominated parliament? You voted in favour of a provision for the President of the Republic, the SYRIZA provision, in which you explicitly stated that you no longer agree to this as a trick, as a trick - I will say that, because that is how it is - you can support this House a completely different position. This is not a sign of institutional seriousness. In essence, however, the proposal you have come to concerning the President of the Republic can be elected by a simple majority - not even a hundred or fifty Members - in fact leads to him making him an unscrupulous majority. It is precisely because our proposals relating to the institutional counterweights, which could balance the power of the Prime Minister, have not been introduced with such a proposal, that you are over-intensively strengthening the pre-central character of the country. I hope that similar games will not be made in the critical vote on 25 November. I say this because you have leaked - it can only be the result of a leak - in friendly newspapers, in 'KATHMERINA', for example, that only those proposals which have received positive treatment from the Committee on Review would be put to the vote. This would be directly unconstitutional, because only plenary can assess and vote - whether positive or negative - on the proposals made by the House. As far as I understand it from the debate with the President of Parliament and from what has been discussed in the Conference of Presidents, these thoughts have been abandoned and indeed all the suggestions of the proposed Parliament will be put to the vote on Monday. But it is not so in procedural terms, it is in the essence of our deep ideological differences and it is in the debate on the substance that the ideological regression of the New Democracy, even in relation to its recent self, 2014. Indeed, the proposal of the Review then tabled - with the signatures not only of Mr Samaras, but also of Mr Mitsotakis - was much closer to European legal culture. He proposed, for example, a referendum on a popular legislative initiative, a way of electing a President of the Republic similar to that of our proposal. Your refusal to vote in favour of an interpretative statement on the religious neutrality of the state, for example, coincides with the attempt to restore the criminalisation of blasphemy against the divine. In short, ladies and gentlemen, the ideological signal you have given to the Review is a foreign signal with European legal culture, a foreign language, even with your own European political family, and certainly not what the country needs today. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-18-S1-commons.u14	Thank you, Mr President. Since the beginning of the process of the Constitutional Review by the House, we have stressed that its main objective was to strengthen the conditions of governmental and political stability, of political regularity, as it has been heard, in order to ensure the uninterrupted implementation of the sovereign anti-grass policy, was to shield the civil system itself, to become more resilient to trivialisations created by this policy, to shield itself mainly against the popular factor, but also to shield itself against its internal contradictions and sometimes threaten its stability. This was the first and great goal for SYRIZA, who took the initiative in the previous House, but also for the New Democracy, regardless of their individual differences, as well as whatever the extent to which this objective is finally achieved. What is the result of this assessment? Firstly, it comes from the proposals of these parties themselves, which in basic articles - I am not saying in all or most - converging. They don't identify them, but they certainly converge. Secondly, it follows from the fact that with previous constitutional revisions and in particular with the 2001 Review, much adjustments have already been made concerning the capitalist economy based on the current needs of this economy. For example, the process of integration of the country into the Euro-Union framework - Articles 28 and 80 of the Constitution with the interpretative statements - that is, this framework, which brought the memoranda of the anti-grassroots measures, which have been implemented and implemented by all the governments of the New Democracy, PASOK and SYRIZA. This framework at the level of the Constitution does not call into question anyone other than the JRC, so what remains is urgent for you on the basis of your own logic is the corresponding adjustments in the field of the political system, the way in which the urban state, its governments, obviously taking into account the experience of the years of the crisis and the memorials, the triumphs caused in the political system, which we of course did not want to be just trifles. We would like it to be much more. During the debates on the Constitution and the Constitutional Review in Parliament it has been heard many times - and today this has been heard by all parties - the following question: What was the resistance of the Constitution in the period of crisis and the period of the memorandum? What is the real meaning of this question? Has the Constitution effectively protected labour rights? Oh, no. That is not the real point, because no civil constitution can effectively protect people's rights, because even if there is some protection in the Constitution for a number of rights - which we of course also pursue, and that is why we have tabled other proposals - at the same time there are clauses in the Constitution, 'walkers', exceptions, the invoking of public interest and public order, the interpretation of the Constitution based on the participation of the country in the European Union, so that at critical times, when required by the needs of the economy and the power of capital, these rights can be literally thrown into the basket of the unfavourable ones. We saw in the years of the crisis how some barbaric class measures were decided to be constitutional and, in many cases, when it was necessary, it was thrown into the basket of unscrupulous ones and the Constitution itself and its provisions. Many examples from all the governments, including from the exceptional legitimisation procedures which all governments have used up and which were completely out of constitutional terms, to the way the memorandums were adopted again by all governments, in contravention of the increased majority of the Constitution. So elsewhere the answer to the question about the resistance of the Constitution lies to you. You want a constitution that in similar circumstances, which will result in mathematically in the future, to shield the system, to ensure political and governmental stability, because you know very well that what we have lived with the crisis is not definitively and irreversibly in the past. You know well that at this time there are colossal developments worldwide. You know well that despite recovery and a good so-called economic climate, which is naturally achieved on the ruins of rights, assessments made by European and international staff are talking about slowing down the economy, about an imminent crisis, perhaps faster than it was expected, so you know very well that Greece will not remain unaffected in such a case and that no management model or mixture, either the so-called neoliberal neoliberal of the New Democracy or the Social Democratic of SYRIZA, can heed these great deadlocks, so that you are also taking your measures to a constitutional and legislative level for the stability of the rotting political system that will surely be threatened in the future. Of course this objective is not confined to constitutional reform alone. It is an overall goal that goes through government initiatives and the current government as well as the previous governments. [[At this point, the First Vice-President of Parliament, Mr Nikitas Kaklamanis, occupyes the President-in-Office of the Council.]] This common goal, as we have said, results from the convergence of the New Democracy, SYRIZA and Change Movement in a number of articles. For example, it arises from your common position on the question of the independent authorities, which have an essential role in many cases, such as the name and only independent authorities - and as precursors of anti-grassroots political choices, as well as your common position on the issue of the election of the President of the Republic, the disconnection of the election of the President of the Republic from the dissolution of the Parliament and the opening of early elections, no matter whether SYRIZA said 'election by the people in the event that the votes are fruitless in Parliament', a proposal which we characterised as very dangerous and voted on by the SYRIZA Members themselves in the House, and that is why it did not pass, while the New Democracy said 'election by a less majority, not just a hundred and a half a Member'. Your original plan even said by draw, which, of course, you withdraw and, in the event of a level playing field, the elder's dominance. This whole proposal, however, to decouple the election of the President of the Republic from the dissolution of the Parliament and the announcement of the elections was essentially a joint proposal, even with different versions. That is why the Rule 32 in the previous House also raised a large majority, more than an eighty-eight Members, and today it can be reviewed by a simple absolute majority of fifty-five Members. Government political stability is also trying to achieve it with other proposals, such as, for example, the SYRIZA proposal - and it is, because it will be put to the vote - on Article 84 of the Constitution and on rejecting motions of censure which are voted on, if there is no agreement on a new Prime Minister, if there is not a new parliamentary majority, and it is an article that exists in the German constitution, which clearly leads to the constitutionalisation of minority governments. A similar proposal was made by the New Democracy for stable four-year governmental cycles, rejected by the previous parliament and leading from another way to the same result. You therefore want a more authoritarian constitution, which will more effectively shield the power of capital. This authoritarianism has too many faces and too many versions. Secondly, this objective was mainly concerned with SYRIZA, who also had the initiative in the previous House, and it was the constitutional reform to be an alibi to its policy as a government. He put forward proposals in the opposite direction to his government. For example, he put forward proposals to protect collective agreements and strikes, when in the collective agreements, as a government, he maintained almost all the main axes that undermine them, with more fundamentally the determination of the minimum wage by the respective Minister of Labour, and the strike brought the law on the strike, '50%+1', which is now continuing and expanding the New Democracy. Of course, you have brought other proposals, which you have painted pro-famous, while in fact they represent the modern-processed capital strategy. For example, your proposal for a decent living, the minimum guaranteed income, such as the New Democracy version, which essentially constitutionalises the logic of the recycling of poverty, the downward equity, the logic of "the poor to pay for the poorer" and, indeed, this is something you can see as a modern, modern version of the social state. Defiance, however, means that people live on the basis of modern needs and on the basis of the possibilities offered by the era, science and technology, and not the management of extreme poverty, and at the same time you have not dared to touch Article 107, an article 'job', which goes from Constitution to Constitution, which guarantees the privileges of armament and foreign capital. The third objective of the Constitutional Review is the so-called morality, the price of the political system, to restore the authority of the political system in the eyes of the people, which has been shaken - and rightly - over all these years of the crisis and the memoranda. This moralization effort combines with the theory of fashioning in the years of the crisis, that for the crisis it is the morality, a theory that acquiescences the economic relations themselves, the market, the competition, with which the crisis and the takeover and involvement are inherent. In this context of moralisation, the proposal for Article 86 and the Law on the liability of ministers, which, while the people's demand - we recall - but also the motto of political forces was that of completely abolishing it, with the proposals made by the SYRIZA and by the New Democracy, this law is not completely abolished, even with certain improvements, such as the abolition of the reduced limitation. It is the interpretative statement proposed by SYRIZA, so that certain offences are directly subject to justice. These, indeed, leave Article 16. What remains in Article 86? It maintains its basic core, namely the assignment to the House of inquiries and public Prosecutors, that the decision to set up a pre-examination committee for the prosecution, the withdrawal of criminal prosecution, will have it again - and with the new version of Rule 86 - the individual parliamentary majority and thus will continue to play the various political games we have seen played at times. The experience of the Committee of Inquiry, which is under way, with the requests for exception, which we have in fact voted against, which are in fact incompatible with the composition and functioning of a parliamentary committee and with all this degeneration which is under way, shows the deadlocks of the current framework and the need for a complete abolition of this article. If you look at the offences investigated by the pre-examination committee for Mr Papagelopoulos and with the new version of Rule 86, these offences will once again be within Parliament's competence: Abuse of power, breach of duty and so on. We voted in the House to change Rule 86. We had made it clear, however, since this Parliament has been fully abolished, and from that we will also determine our final vote. In addition to Article 86, as a JRC, however, in the constitutional review process, we have tabled a number of proposals for the protection and expansion of popular and democratic rights, the trade unions, the sovereign rights of the country, to abolish a series of anachronisms that exist in the Constitution, without, of course, being illusory that there can be a democratic or progressive constitution within the current power of capital. Such examples were, among other things, our proposals for the revision of Articles 27 and 28 of the Constitution, which, today, allow the exercise of the country's sovereign rights to be restricted. We have proposed that the provisions which guarantee the supremacy of European Union law should be reviewed against national law and, indeed, against the Constitution itself. We have proposed to change the provisions that allow foreign military bases to be installed in the country, which, of course, both SYRIZA and New Democracy and other parties have rejected, because you inspire what the Minister of National Defence of New Democracy said a few days ago, that in the past years the Greeks have bleeded to the side of the Americans and NATO and will do the same in the future. For this unacceptable statement, the provocative statement, which turns back many years, was unspeakable from all the other parties. You have also rejected our proposals for the effective safeguarding of a number of social rights, the amendment of Article 18, so that the country's mineral wealth, social goods, public areas in mountainous, saint, archaeological sites can in no way be granted to third parties for exploitation. You refused to adopt or overrun with semi-metres, even some overripe demands of urban modernisation, such as the complete separation of State and Church, which has been launched for two centuries in some countries and has been carried out in other decades. And when we talk about full separation, we mean full separation rather than the caricature that SYRIZA brought. We mean the removal of the prevailing religion in the Constitution, which has legal and ideological consequences, as we also mean the abolition of paragraph 2 of Article 16, which states that education must promote religious conscience. You have even denied the abolition - what I said before - of certain 'job' articles existing in the Greek Constitution, such as Article 48, which refers to a reasonable internal enemy or Article 107, concerning the constitutional safeguarding of the privileges of armaments and foreign capital. You rejected all of this without a debate. Not, of course, that we expected you to accept them, because your strategy, your strategy and the current rulings and the previous ones, the rulings yesterday, do not even fit the slightest discrepancies from this policy, nor even the elementary changes of urban modernisation. We will bring these proposals back and now, at least as far as the articles to be revised are concerned, knowing, of course, that you will again reject them because your own logic for constitutional revision is in a completely different direction and your joint rejection of the JRC's proposals is an element of your policy. We will try through the debate to show that the defence and expansion of people's rights, in order to match these with the modern needs, requires deeper changes, radical changes in the economy, power, overthrowing today's constitution. It is assumed that the developments that will be under way in the coming years will cause new triumphs, new cracks, new instability in the urban political system. We say that this instability must not only be feared, but must make use of it, expand it as far as it goes through and for the benefit of the people, in order to prevent measures at its expense, divert what it can, create breaches and, above all, create a connection and conditions for a more comprehensive conflict with the current rotten system. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-18-S1-commons.u15	Thank you too, Mr. Gioka. The reason is the special speaker from the Greek Solution Mr. Konstantinos Hetas. Here, you have the floor.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-18-S1-commons.u16	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, with the national interest the Greek Solution is taking an active part in the top parliamentary reform process of the Constitution. We have developed our main proposals for major issues relating to referendums, the need for active involvement of citizens in the governance of the country, the vote on the results and the role of the President of the Republic. There are no anachronistic provisions and populism in the Constitution. The revision of the country's Charter should form the basis on which we will build the answers to the serious problems of Greek citizens. But through this process it became clear to me that sterile confrontation has now passed on to the political DNA of the New Democracy, SYRIZA and the Change Movement - PASOK. Unfortunately, many times - usually empty seats by MPs - witnessed micro-political classifications aimed at creating impressions about such a serious issue. With the Constitution, however, we should not be experimenting. Through the Revision of the Constitution, the country's city is reconstructed and the state is remodeled in its fundamental features. But what are we waiting for? What is the Greek citizen expecting from the Review of our country's highest rule of law? That's the question. Firstly, real will to improve the articles of the Constitution aimed at strengthening citizens' rights and society itself. Secondly, the amendment of the constitutional provisions should aim at the final aim and result in the problems facing the citizen being solved. Thirdly, real dialogue with all parties, far from micro-political purposes and interests. Fourthly, an exchange of views and arguments with a view to finding the best and not a theoretical demonstration of legal knowledge. Fifthly, the involvement of the citizen himself in the revision process, so that it is not something foreign and something indifferent to him. Sixteen and last but not least, a review that would turn to the citizen himself and aim to decentralise powers. But what have we seen? A constitutional review without daring, but more thorough, since the previous parliament did not dare to touch fundamental articles. This means that this House can only modify what the previous Parliament has sent it. Even during the preparation of the constitutional provisions, it was heard that the revision process should be stopped and that is why the articles that took the green light for revision are very few and thus this revision will ultimately not have any practical advantage. We are wasting time, that is, since this review is finally done for the eyes of the world. Indeed, critical articles of the Constitution that deal with the start of the same review by the citizens, the establishment of the constitutional court, the abolition of the notorious Article 86 of the Constitution on the preferential treatment of ministers, private universities, whether they were not even qualified for revision or considered a reviewer, but only in a few paragraphs. But the importance of public participation has not been recognized either by opening up power to the citizens. A characteristic example of this is the election of the President of the Republic directly by Greek citizens. The President of the Republic is the supreme ruler of the Greek state, he is the regulator of the regime, he is the Chief of the Armed Forces. It is worth giving him the authority he deserves, both as an institution and as a representative of the country. The proposal of the Greek Solution is stable and is as follows: The President of the Republic is directly elected by Greek citizens following proposals from candidates, from ten MPs or from thirty thousand citizens. It will be a disastrous mistake, ladies and gentlemen, to go to an election of the highest institution of the State, the President of the Republic, on the basis of only the choice of the Prime Minister in question. It's not right. It alters the meaning of democracy. During the Constitutional Review - I said this to the Committee - in 1986, which considered whether the President of the Republic could appoint the Prime Minister of the country, a PASOK Member had said sceptics: "The President of the Republic in question cannot appoint a Prime Minister." We said, however, that we should not get to the other end, that the Prime Minister with his government appointing a President of the Republic his gardener, his friend or friend, or the one who would give him various ex-institutional centres of power. The vote on the results, another extremely important issue, of course, is not a new one. We all agree that the time has finally come to recognise the right to vote on Greek expatriates, that is, self-evident. However, the whole issue is commonly characterised by a legal complexity, which is greater than that which one might assume by the naked eye. Personally, I would like to believe that we will all play with open papers on this sensitive subject. Let's go, ladies and gentlemen, to the private universities. This is the big loser's chance. The establishment and operation of private universities would achieve, on the one hand, the repatriation of hundreds of thousands of Greek scientists and students, on the other hand, the attraction of thousands of foreign students, who would study in a country with a particular culture and sought cognitive objects. If that is what Great Britain can do, since Cyprus has been able to do so, I do not understand why Greece cannot do so. The only obstacle is the inability to overcome the modesty that characterizes the political forces. It is incomprehensible in the 21st century that the study of tens of thousands of Greeks in private universities and recognized by foreign universities, while at the same time denying the revision of Article 16. The opportunity we throw into the trash is huge. The turnover of private universities amounts to unthinkable figures, in billions of euros. For example, with revenues of 3 billion from the private universities with our pilot Cyprus - probably for Greece to be higher the number - we would not have to discover patents to reduce, for example, HISPIA. So that's a long lost chance. As regards the dispersion of power, in Greece the dispersion of power is an inappropriate objective. However, it should not be an inappropriate objective, because the dispersion of power is successfully applied to many - not to say to most - countries in the civilised world. In the Greek Solution we firmly believe that the dispersion of power is the best way to limit corruption and complexity. The country needs a broad dispersion of power. Is it not, then, worth wondering why this dispersion of power is not taking place? Where does this power spread stumble? Does the refusal of the central authority to share power? Did not some want and do not want to accept limiting their own authority? So the dilemma is centralized or diffuse power? The Greek Solution is clearly against centralism and in favour of decentralisation. But not just administrative bureaucratic decentralisation, but the diffusion of the decision-making centre itself. We are against globalisation and over-concentration of powers and in favour of a national society with identity and autonomy. We are against a loose passive society and we are in favour of a dynamic society. We are against the image of the citizen who obeys state power and in favour of the active citizen involved in the formulation of decisions concerning him. We are against centralised power and in favour of decentralisation of power. We believe in a dynamic democracy that will actually express the popular will and not a fossil democracy that cannot meet the demands of the times and the will of the Greek people. This Review, however, - the fourth one - has unfortunately not been able to stand up to the circumstances. He was unable to go over and stay away from political purposes and constitutional populism. Secondly, it upgrades Parliament, by making it possible for the opposition to Rule 68 with a proposal of ten Members and a hundred and twenty present to set up committees of inquiry, which it is developing into a powerful tool of parliamentary control, thereby restoring the principle of controls and balances. Thirdly, it restores citizens' confidence in politicians by limiting parliamentary immunity to Article 62 only in the performance of their duties and the abolition of the amortisation deadline in Article 86(3), which equates ministers criminally to ordinary citizens. Thus, it ensures governmental stability and respect for the popular verdict. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-18-S1-commons.u35	Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, the initiative to revise the current constitution is linked to the need to formulate stables so that the country can be shielded, so that it does not fall back into bankruptcy and the grand memoranda, but also to the need to have a flexible basic framework of constitutional rules that allows constitutional institutions to adapt to modern changing conditions. The Greek Constitution is strict and is subject to review in two phases. Since the Constitution is the main law of the state, wider consents are required for its review, which in turn presupposes and entail compositions and duration. This is also reflected in the review procedure which extends two parliamentary periods, namely the consensus to last for two Bulgarians, where there may also be different majorities as in this case. At the same time, each Review has an ideological political mark, progressive or conservative. The nature of the bond depends not only on the majority parties, but also on the demands of the wider context. The period after the fall of the dictatorship required gradual advances and choices, which were partly reflected in the 1975 Constitution, despite the fact that the country's governance was a conservative party. Today, after the bankruptcy, the memorial loot and then the exit of the country from the memorials, the Revision of the Constitution requires progressive choices that respond to the need to decapitate from imposed Memorandum regulations and burdens, but also to the modern requirements that require progressive, shallow responses. In this Review House, the Revision of the Constitution is completed. The proposed Parliament has passed the articles to be revised and the essential political direction to be taken to them. The question is whether this Review House is bound by the direction of reviewing the articles proposed to be revised or completely free to carry out their revision without taking into account the direction and giving them any content. Revision of the Constitution is a single procedure. In this light, both within the context of the grammar, the systematic terminology and the logic of the Constitution is the first version. As a Review, the two Boulevards constitute one unity. Otherwise for the Review, two Vultures would not be foreseen. The Constitution would be sufficient in one, which would decide according to the order of business, both on the revised provisions and on the direction, their essential content. The proposal for a revision of specific provisions - Articles contains a rationale for an evaluation crisis, a justification for the need to revise it, and therefore defines the direction in which the substantive content of the revision is to be formulated, without specifying the way, the wording of the provisions to be revised. This is also provided for in Article 110(2) of the Constitution, which states: ‘The need to revise the Constitution shall be determined by a decision of the Parliament. This Decision lays down in particular the revised provisions.’ Consequently, the direction of the revised provisions of the Constitution which has been awaited by the Proposed Parliament must, in our view, form the basis of the Review by the Revision House, so that there can also be a wider consensus that requires compositions and duration. The fact that on the two Bulgarians, proposing and revising, majorities are different does not mean that no consensus should be sought. This has been done in such a way, thankfully, to the committee responsible under the responsibility of the governing party. The exception to the principle of impunity, of course, is what is expressly stated in Article 62(4) for self-determination offences. Another basic difference between the irresponsible and the irresponsible is that the latter, i.e. the irresponsible, has no time, whereas the irresponsible is only valid as long as the term of the parliamentary term. This is an important difference between the irresponsible and the irresponsible. I shall say two words on Rule 86, and I shall close, Mr President, and thank you. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-18-S1-commons.u63	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the revision of the Constitution is a rare and top-level process, where Parliament is solely responsible for it, and each one of us must consider the particular weight of its vote, because the revision of the Constitution is not a mere amendment to a common law. It is the change in the country's Charter, which regulates the functioning of the constitution, which guarantees the functioning of the country's democratic system of governance and, of course, regulates the relationship between democracy and the rule of law. The 1975 Constitution, the pioneer for its time, democratic and liberal, is the longest-lived constitution of the Greek state. Forty-five years it has been - and it has been - the foundation of the most quality and stable parliamentary democracy which our country has ever experienced. Why? Because it is a constitution that is strict and at the same time open. It is strict because it can protect itself against attempts to circumvent it - Article 110 - and, of course, protect the hard core of our national and political worthy system. And it is open, because it can adapt to modern trends, without causing a alteration of the constitutional framework of our country. In our time, where everything is questioned and redefined, the Revision of the Constitution could be a golden opportunity to protect our national and cultural identity, reconstruction and state modernisation. Unfortunately, this opportunity in the largest part of it has been lost. The refusal then proposed parliament condemns the country to remain behind Europe for many years. This was considered necessary in order not to address issues of unconstitutionality, which is due to come to parliament in the next few days. Of course, our proposal, the New Democracy proposal, is well known and remains well known. Greeks from abroad could vote at their place, as they would have been able to vote if they traveled to Greece, in their constituency, freely and universally. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-18-S1-commons.u69	Ladies and gentlemen, the text of the proposed provisions is the result of a fruitful and meaningful dialogue within the Review Committee. I begin with the proposal of the New Democracy to add paragraph 4 to Article 54 of the Constitution on the question of the vote of Greek voters abroad. The purpose of the proposed provision is to make it possible to energise the participation of Greeks abroad in the parliamentary elections and to define at constitutional level the main axes of the legislation. In this way, firstly, we relieve the legislator of all sorts of reservations and endless discussions about the possible unconstitutionality of legislation and, secondly, we allow the legislator to move in comfort and power, by making legislative arrangements from a range of possible legislative options. We therefore propose the following framework within which the legislator can move in order to activate the institution of the vote of Greeks abroad in the parliamentary elections: firstly, the exercise of the electoral right, such as the personal presence in an election station or the presence in the country for a certain period of time cumulatively in the past. Secondly, there may be mandatory positions in the predominant ballot in Greeks of the resultant Hellenism. Thirdly, there can be provision for the measurement of the Greeks' vote abroad in all the constituencies, or exclusively at the level of dominance. Ladies and gentlemen, we have the national obligation to activate the Greeks' vote, not just for symbolic reasons, but firstly because the Constitution itself protects it. And I am not talking about Article 51(3) of the Constitution, which provides for that possibility. Firstly, I am referring to the statuteal and non-revised provision of Article 1(3), which concerns the very form of the constitution and states that all the powers of the constitution exist in favour of the people, therefore of the nation, namely the broader Greekism. Secondly, I am referring to the provision of Article 51(2) of the Constitution, which stipulates that the MPs represent the nation, so also the Greeks abroad, so also through foreign parliamentarians. But now the institution of the vote of Greeks abroad is imposed by our own history. It is of primary and fundamental importance that we keep the Hellenism united and make it a social, responsible and active person in the face of the geopolitical challenges that we face. Greece has always been, ladies and gentlemen, and will remain something much greater than the sum of the history of the people of only one region. We here and now, having the honour of being members of the Greek Parliament, must show responsibility, consensual and practical in this matter. The need for the proper functioning of the constitution is the revision of Article 32(4) and the election of the President of the Republic by a simple majority of the Parliament without prior recourse to elections. The objective for the election in the position of the President of the Republic of broad acceptance and consent shall of course not be abandoned. However, the elimination of the possibility of provoking elections from the election process of the President of the Republic and hence the party work of the institution gives essential meaning to the three votes with an increased majority required by our Constitution and directs the political forces represented in the House to achieve the first vote of the broadest possible consensus, away from circumstances and purposes. I will continue with the review of Rule 68(2) and the proposed setting-up of committees of inquiry by a decision of the two-fifths of all Members. After more than forty years, that is, since the 1975 Constitution, where the decision to set up committees of inquiry is recognised as a right and I would say rather a privilege of self-control of the governmental majority, for the first time today expressly and clearly, as is appropriate in a constitutional text and in a modern Parliament accepting minority rights, the right to set up committees of inquiry and minority groups is granted, since the proposal is voted in favour of all of the Members. The setting-up of committees of inquiry with the power to monitor the government's majority, as it was up to now, becomes an essential right to minority voting and parliamentary control that can be exercised in the work of the government is strengthened. The revision at this point is in the interests of the functioning of Parliament. Equally important is the proposed revision of Article 86(3) that the abolition of the special deadline for prosecuting the Ministers should be allowed, a provision which has been introduced to prevent charges being perpetuated against the Ministers, but unfortunately it has led to the irresponsibleness of the ministers. The short-term amortisation period, not only, does not allow the House to effectively carry out the work conferred on it by Article 86, but it has created the image of a general privileged treatment of the ministers, which seems to enjoy a peculiar duty-free regime. The abolition of this deadline and the validity of the rules on limitations applicable to all citizens strengthen the functioning of the House, even if it is called upon to seek responsibilities against the Ministers, but also to strengthen the sense of public confidence in the functioning of the institutions. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-18-S1-commons.u73	A top parliamentary procedure is taking place in plenary today, after a hard work that has been done before the House, as well as at the meetings of the committees of the Revision Parliament, which is concerned with the Constitution. In other words, the Constitution creates a form of certain limits, but within which political confrontation and political conflict can transform contents in one or the other direction, depending on the interests they sometimes gain in political battle. If, then, we see the Constitution through this perspective and through this prism, we realise that the consensus on the Revision of the Constitution is neither political nor moral selfishness. This type of void Constitution, which is just a form of defining the organisational terms of the political conflict, is the Constitution in which SYRIZA can never agree. This procedural approach, however, is neither moral nor politically neutral. In other words, it cannot be the only legitimisation of a political decision to be the process. On the other hand, the legal function of the procedure, the production of the law or the legal function of the executive to produce regulations, are limited to the substantive weightings which the Constitution makes, the weightings that arise precisely through the war conflict and confrontation. Some examples of this are absolutely enlightening. As regards, for example, the architecture of the political system, all the previous years, regardless of their political and ideological positions, and regardless of whether they wanted to retreat, there have been suffocating pressures on the part of institutions, which do not draw legal power from popular sovereignty, they do not draw their legitimacy immediately from the Greek people. So the political system needs to consider this to see how it shields governments, parliaments from this type of suffocating pressures, given the existing boundaries of this constitution, which can reach a certain point in protecting precisely the government's choices of democracy, etc., given the unfavourable global correlation of power. My proposal concerns the provisions of Article 101(4) concerning the structural criteria for legislative planning and implementing practices for the most vulnerable regions in our country, islands and mountains, which claim their equality through their specificities, which, from their weaknesses in a structured development organisation, can be transformed into their dynamic prospects. The Review Committee seemed to accept my proposal, but which completely unnecessarily did not print in the final proposal in the proposed provisions. Today, once again, I call on the House to look at the essential prospect of island and mountainous Greece in the struggles of the people who hold the modern Thermopoils, by tabling the proposal to supplement Article 101(4), in a way that will only help with two key words in the future. Firstly, the regulatory design of the legislative initiative, secondly the executive crisis of the administration, and thirdly, the assessment of the legality of the judiciary which, until now, together with only the voluntary perception of good legislation and of a systematic constitutional crisis, can be activated and, with the new provisions of Articles 57 and 58 on good lawmaking of the executive state of L.4622/19, they have a partially binding dimension. This was then again the case in order not to abolish the relevant provision on islands. I would therefore like to say that the principles which I propose to integrate are the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality, which are in force in the whole of our single constitutionalism, European and national, but require their more active, positive and also depository presence in the guarantees of the state. I therefore propose that the provision be re-drafted as follows: ‘The joint legislator and the administration, when acting regulatoryly, are obliged to take into account the particular circumstances of the island and mountain regions, while ensuring their balanced development in causal link with the principles of proportionality and subsidiarity’. It is a wording that I think covers more fully the original proposal in the Proposing Parliament, which refers more generally to full social and economic development, but which also comes in a straightforward legal balance both with paragraph 1 on decentralised governance under Article 101 and with the provisions of the relevant Article 106(1). This provision also takes account of all those measures for the use of the sources of national wealth, from the atmosphere and underground deposits to promote regional development and in particular the promotion of the economy of mountain and island regions. The minimum guaranteed income is a social indicator and a fourth pillar of protection. The other three are the labour market, family and social protection. When the previous three fail to intervene effectively, the state must activate the minimum guaranteed income and compensate for its failure. The minimum guaranteed income should not act as a trap of poverty and have a passive role. In increasing the number of MPs from twelve to fifteen, so that parties can self-regulation place themselves in election positions and candidates from abroad. And in that the vote will take place in the local embassies and consulates of Greece and will require a personal presence of Greeks abroad. Thirdly, the separation of the procedure for the election of the President of the Republic from the parliamentary elections. The highest rule with the limited regulatory powers, which we all know, will be elected by the House with an absolute relative majority. This change is imperative, as in the past there has been a laborious use of the procedure at issue with a view to party purposes and electoral strategies. Fourthly, in the equation of the military judges with the other judges, so that, according to the European Court of Human Rights, the guarantees of independent functioning of military justice can be claimed, by removing it from the outdated logic of the military courts. Fifthly, the strictness of the immunity of the Member who is shielded only in the performance of his duties, and the provision of Article 86(3) is abolished, thus equating the Minister with the ordinary citizens in the violation of criminal offences. This is how people are told that they must once again trust the political system, while at the same time it is also a strong measure to combat corruption. Sixth, in the setting up of a committee of inquiry, following a proposal by ten opposition MPs and a hundred and twenty Members present, thus shifting the examination into a strong parliamentary control tool and upgrading the control role of the national Parliament. Seventhly, to facilitate the election of the administration of the independent authorities. After the adoption and application of the law on the performance of the State, the administrations of these authorities will be able to be elected by 3/5 of the Conference of Presidents so that they do not remain head-on. Finally, the Review Committee has sometimes come up with a simple and sometimes increased majority in seven key points of proposed constitutional changes. I'll stand on four of them: First point. The committee proposed in Article 21(1) a minimum guaranteed income scheme to support the Greek family, which is the main cell of our society. This intervention by the New Democracy also stresses the principle of a social market economy in the Greek Constitution. Second point. The procedure for the election of the President of the Republic shall be separated from the parliamentary elections in accordance with Article 32(4) of the Constitution. That is to say, the election of the House by an absolute or relevant majority, so that the people's mandate for a government is not interrupted deliberately for the election of a President. This prevents the highest rule from being elected to work and, at the same time, is a guarantee of political stability. Unfortunately, ladies and gentlemen, in the recent past, we have been led to elections on the occasion of the election of the President of the Republic to draw up opposing political benefits from opposition parties, but with disastrous consequences for the country. The first was 2009, with the result that the new government that came out of the elections led the country to the International Monetary Fund and two memoranda. The second in 2015, resulting in the new government loading the country with a third memorandum, larger than everyone, of EUR 100 billion, and a loss of EUR 200 billion. Third point. The immunity of the Member referred to in Article 62 of the Constitution, which is shielded only in the performance of his duties and which abolishes the amortisation deadline laid down in Article 86(3) by equating the Minister with all citizens as a whole to the limitation of criminal offences. This regulation aims at combating corruption at the same time and restoring citizens' confidence in the political system. Fourth point. The selection of members of independent authorities shall be facilitated in accordance with Article 101a of the Constitution, which, after its revision, shall be elected by 3/5 of the Conference of Presidents. The main reason for this change is that the authorities are not headless. Allow me, as far as the independent authorities are concerned, to put forward some thoughts. First of all, I insist that the possibility of establishing new principles should be restricted. There's already a lot of them. I have set an example in the Committee on Review, which I have referred to very quickly. In 2012-2013 the Memorandum of Understanding provided for the establishment of independent authorities or regulators for water, ports, motorways, airports. As Deputy Minister of Development and Infrastructure, then I took the initiative to avoid creating a new water principle. On the contrary, we have strengthened the responsibilities of the existing Water Secretariat. Neither a new independent motorway authority was subsequently created but only one service was created at the Ministry of Infrastructure on road charges. In other words, it has proved in practice that it is not always necessary to set up a new authority. I conclude, Mr President, by saying that I believe that Parliament's control over the independent authorities should be substantially strengthened. Thank you very much.

Article 3 therefore simply expresses the history and timelessness of the constitutional text, without conflicting with religious freedom, since the non-revisionable provision of Article 13(1) of the Constitution clearly states that the enjoyment of individual and civil rights does not depend on the religious beliefs of anyone. This proposal has clear advantages to the naughty mosaic of benefits. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-18-S1-commons.u130	Thank you, Mr President, Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, today we are starting the top parliamentary process of the Revision of the Constitution, a main political process drawn up by the National Representation and implemented by the executive. The Constitution of Greece is the Charter on which the formation of the entire legislation of the rights and obligations of the citizen is based, the organization and the basic rules of operation of the State and of the institutions. The vote of the first Greek constitution by the National Assembly of Epidaurus in January 1822 was the crown of the political history of the younger Greece at national level. Since then, thirteen Reviews have been carried out in our country. We are now called on to amend a number of articles, such as those passed by the Proposed House, which - I would remind you - had dealt with a hundred and fifty-four articles, provisions and paragraphs to be revised. Of these fifty - four, however, finally the forty - nine took the required number of votes to be revised.
