ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-18-S1-commons.u132	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the debate on the Review of a Constitution is a top moment for Parliament. At the same time as we feel the burden of responsibility, we must realise, of course, that a Constitution is nothing more, much more, than a wish and a little less than a joint declaration of intent. Because, in the end, no Constitution can give citizens of a country - such as ours, such as any other - rights, when the state itself is a substitute, no freedom can be attributed by a state of seal. We will remind a court whose decisions simply cannot be implemented, as is the case in that country. First, it is necessary to establish popular sovereignty and then to write a Constitution that confirms it, verify it, launch it, guide it. Over the last ten years in our country, basically our national independence has been destroyed, our own state. Our ministries remind themselves of Swiss cheese, which is missing, and the important decisions that should be made on them are not there. So we are talking about a humiliation of the existing constitution, which we are now reviewing. This does not mean that our work today is not important. This does not mean that the symbolisation of the Constitution, of its articles, is of no importance and is not a source of optimism, hope for the citizens, since we keep the order of magnitude in our minds, what we are trying to do. You know, we in Mera25 dislike patrioticism and always as internationalists and citizens of the world, we think we should learn from other countries and other peoples. But this is a moment when you will allow us as Mera25 to say that democracy in its true sense has been nowhere in the world, only in ancient Athens and for very few decades. In ancient Athens the term was defined by Aristotle. Democracy is the rule of the poor, because the poor by definition are in the majority. He may not have been a democrat himself, Aristotle himself, not in love with the concept of democracy, being held up by aristocracy, but the definition was crucial. And if we think about it, despite the fact that there was an exclusion of women, the exclusion of the brokers, the exclusion of slaves, yet in those few decades in ancient Athens we had a unique moment in the history of humanity, a moment when civilians, citizens of poor people, had an equilibrium and an equal vote in the Church of the Municipality. On the contrary, Western democracy, representative democracy does not have its roots in this idea, in the idea described so nicely by Aristotle. It has its roots in Magna Charta, where Magna Charta - not to be forgotten - was a treaty between the barons and the king, which guaranteed the rights of the barons, not the people, to the king, one of whom was the right to have slaves, whom the king would not touch. The development of the British Parliament, which was the first Parliament in Western Europe, of representative democracy, is beginning there. It's starting with Magna Charta. This is the first time a serious constitution has been written, a constitution that still guides the authors of constitutions worldwide. It is the Constitution of the United States, which, of course, started with the federal texts, with the 'federal papers' before writing the same thing. What do we see throughout this process, especially in the footnotes of the U.S. Constitution? We see a huge effort by the municipality to exclude from democracy, the municipality to advise, to consult politicians, to have where and where citizens have a vote, at regular intervals, but at the same time, the citizens, the many - and indeed the Americans - have used the ancient Greek phrase 'hoi Polloi', many - to be outside the decisions, not to risk the aristocracy of the owners of many. In other words, what we have seen for decades has been a democracy, a democratic city in the West, which is declining, which leads citizens to alienation, with ever lower rates of participation in the common. This for us, at Mera25, is not a failure of this representative process or democracy, but it is part of its design. But I will say it once again: the Constitution as a text is of great importance. One word, a small article of it can give power to democrats, power to popular masses, power to the weak, so that they make a difference. Let me remind you in the 1960s - I don't need to remind you, you remember, but I will mention it - an article, in 1.1.4, in that Constitution, unclear, authoritarian, unacceptable, was capable of donating, giving strength, strengthening protesters in favour of democracy. 1.1.4 was the beginning of the Polytechnic School that we celebrated yesterday on the streets of Athens with a truly grand gathering. And since I am talking about the Polytechnics, ladies and gentlemen, from all the wings, I would like to draw your attention to the fact that the Polytechnics is actually living in Hong Kong today. Right now that we're talking about the five hundred students of Hong Kong Polytechnic are trapped in the Hong Kong Polytechnic, just like was going on here in November of '73, with the police enclaved them. At the moment when we are talking to the Members of the Greek Parliament, without any willingness to participate in this, I think that, especially during the debate on freedoms and rights, which gives and offers a Constitution, our minds should be in Hong Kong, to these students of the Hong Kong Polytechnic who are re-creating, as they have to do every generation, conditions of combat, fight for democracy. Ladies and gentlemen, a country is judged, like a family, by the way in which it treats its weakest members, which is why it is of fundamental importance for us in this debate - and we will develop this in the days ahead - to protect the weak, at least verbally, at least through the articles of the Constitution. The Constitutions of representative urban democracies over the last 100 years make a distinction between negative freedoms and positive freedoms. Negative freedoms are, in fact, an attempt to protect the privileged, the right of the owner not to take his home - unless he is a banker! - the right of the owner to have a strong fence that protects him from the poor, from the thief, from the thief, from the state itself if he falls into the hands of a progressive force that wants to redistribute wealth. When the conflict between democrats and aristocrats took place at a philosophical level, on a rhetoric level, at political level in the Church of the Municipality, what was the most interesting part of that conflict? The democrats were against the elections and the aristocrats were in favour of the elections. The argument of the democrats against the elections is that the aristocrats always have the way to influence the elections even through their good rhetoric that they have acquired, because they had the money to ensure the best rhetoric, teachers for their children. We propose that there should be a permanent body, a DISCIP, consisting of 1/3 of contributors, university teachers, high school teachers, 1/3 citizens, just like the jury and 1/3 appointed by the parties, the experts representing the parties. I believe that the regions should be one of the levers of development in the region, but they should respond and should be interviewed by councils, where elected representatives are represented, but at the same time there should be contributors, who will interrogate, ask questions, hear questions from experts and finally make proposals that citizens will then choose which of these proposals they want, through a process of consultation and referendum. We propose the same for the President of the Republic. On the one hand, the current situation cannot continue, on the other hand, we cannot allow the President of the Republic to be elected from one hundred and fifty Members only. We do not want universal suffrage, because that then alters the essence of the culture, from the point of view that the President of the country will have received many more votes than the Prime Minister, but it will not have any more competence than the current President of the Republic. We propose something very simple, one vote in Parliament. If we manage and agree between us and two hundred MPs vote for one candidate or one candidate, otherwise a PIC is elected, where the MPs are one/3, six hundred others are confiscated and they decide who the President of the Republic is. I would remind you that the great Thomas Jefferson, one of the greatest praisers of freedom, had slaves, whom he did not even agree to release after death. So is the Soviet Union. Can I remind you? Anyone who reads the Constitution of the Soviet Union can only admire an excellent text. But the lack of democratic procedures actually turned it into an empty shirt. Here, we must combine realism with a text that highlights our values to the extent that they may be common. And where they cannot be common, to highlight the values that arise through democratic processes. Napoleon said a Constitution must be short and inconceivable. We in Mera25 believe that a Constitution must be short and crystalline. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-18-S1-commons.u156	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the SYRIZA government took the initiative in the previous parliament to move on to the Revision of the Constitution, since since the last Review of 2008, serious subversive events had taken place, with the memoranda that the country has known since 2010. Our goal was immediately after leaving the memorandum, in August 2018, to move the country into a large section with the institutional change of the supreme law, the Constitution. And this institutional change was aimed at the armouring and foundation of the democratic culture, the upgrading of the credibility of the political system, the distinction of the State from the Church, the protection of public goods, the safeguarding of social and individual rights. Unfortunately, the elections of 7 July reversed the direction of the spirit and letter of the Review of the Constitution that we had started, by way of derogation from the viewpoint of many valid constitutionalists, who argue that the proposed parliament, namely the previous parliament, also defines the direction. Thus, what they received from a hundred and fifty one votes to seventy nine votes, and now a hundred eighty votes were required to vote in practically, they were thrown into the ballot of the useless. I shall mention at least eighteen articles which were rejected by the government majority and which, in our opinion, constitute - and they constitute - the quintany of a progressive constitution, which is seen in the future rather than in the past. Article 3(1) and (2) refers to the religious neutrality of the Greek state with recognition of the prevailing religion, without recognition of official state religion, a step towards separating the role of the Church and the State. Unfortunately, the new government's majority has resurrected and annulled this whole case, as well as the fundamental European acquis. Article 5(2): Ensure the absolute protection of life, the honor and the freedom of citizens, without discrimination on grounds of sex and sexual orientation. Another removal and overthrow of the European acquis. Article 13(5): Occupation of government officials and civil servants with political oath. It is a precedent of the previous article. Article 21(3): State guarantee for the right to health and the obligation of the State to provide universal access to the National Health System. Add a new paragraph to Article 21, which has of course also been rejected: Ensuring basic social goods such as water, electricity and distribution networks in public service and public control. Article 22(1): Protection of the right to work by the State, equal pay for equal work, regardless of sex and age. And we continued in Article 22(2): Enforcement of working conditions, collective agreements and collective bargaining. Paragraph 4 in the same article: Prohibition of a job and award of special laws. In the case of war, confiscation, theory and risk to public health, there should be specific legislation. Prohibition of servicing services as a measure to deal with the strike. Article 22(5): Establishment of a single, public, redistributive universal social security system. This whole article is the quintuosity of the impending and defending basic rights of workers and citizens, against the arbitraryness of market immunity and neoliberalism. Article 25(3): Jurisdiction and due diligence in the exercise of individual and social rights. Article 28(2): Clearance by a referendum of international agreements or treaties in the event of a transfer of sovereign rights of the State. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-18-S1-commons.u161	Mr Syrigos has the floor from New Democracy.
ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-18-S1-commons.u162	Thank you very much, Mr President. Mr Ministers, ladies and gentlemen, the requirements for a modern constitution are now radically different from those of the 1970s and 1980s. At that time, it was a question of creating a secure democratic culture and ensuring the individual rights of citizens. On this subject the authors of the 1975 Constitution have succeeded. We are living the most stable and longest period of democracy in the history of the modern Greek state, with smooth changes in power completely different between political parties. The demands today, however, are different. A state must operate in a globalised world and in a highly competitive international environment. In the globalised era it is important that a state should be able to exploit its powers and, above all, to adapt to developments. This possibility has been described as ‘purpose’, is the translation of the English word ‘capacity’. So the questions raised in relation to the Constitution are three: to be able to recognise the problems of the time, to address them either by resolving them or proposing alternative positions and to be treated democratically and acceptably by the majority of society. Thus, the conditions are created for both prosperity and wealth and for the protection of the individual in his everyday life. Thus, Greece can take part in international procedures that concern it directly, to ensure its financial needs and security. The question is often asked whether the pursuit of a powerful state is anachronism in a globalised era. The answer is that economic operators still prefer investment to powerful states rather than weak and insecure states, which cannot guarantee the safety and stability of their system. What is our problem with the current constitution? His strictness. 'Strict' in constitutional law means the cases in which the constitutional texts, which are amended or repealed by a special procedure, are more difficult than the corresponding procedure for common rules of law. When do we have a problem? When the Constitution contains very detailed constitutional arrangements and there are extremely strict provisions on its revision. We now come to an Review, which is anemic and moves to the least common denominator. The proposed articles do not contain any preposterous strategy for a comprehensive revision of the Constitution. When we are talking today about fundamental fundamental concepts, such as the election of President of the Republic or the responsibility of Ministers, it is like addressing first-year students under constitutional law. In this sense, my colleague's view is confirmed at the university headquarters, Evans Hatzidis, who has noticed that when we have consensual procedures, we have reached the minimum common denominator, while instead of the constitutional proposals from a political force - as happened in the 1911 and 1975 constitutions - they are conducting sectors both in the Constitution and in society. What would be the points on which we should advance more boldly? The first, in my opinion, would be the way in which the highest judges are chosen. It must be upheld by the will of the government majority. Second point: Greece in the ninety-nine years of its free life has failed to demonstrate that it can operate an effective state-of-the-art bureaucratic mechanism. It is an issue that we have never dealt with in the Constitutions before. A third point relates to the protection of the citizen in 2019. We are usually referring to issues of individual and social rights. Let me tell you another example. The question of explicitly abolishing the possibility of successive extensions of the time limit for the limitation of the public’s tax claims in the context of the constitutionally regulated prohibition on retroactive taxation. It is a minimum denominator and a rule on legal certainty. And since I am referring to legal certainty, the court has to control the constitutionality of the laws. He has employed the legal world, he has employed the citizens, he has employed legal science every time a review process begins. In our country the tradition of the diffuse concrete and incidental constitutionality check has prevailed. A characteristic example of the law on insurance - which took the name of the then Minister, Mr Katrugalou - which had to be passed three years after his vote to judge the judgment of the anti-constitutional provisions. It's obvious we need to establish a constitutional court. I believe that this is also the question of setting up a special section in the Council of State for the rapid trial of major public procurement and private investment cases. The last point is the rationalisation of the organisation and functioning of the independent authorities. One step is taken with this Review. It makes it easier to choose the members of the independent authorities, but we need to look at it a little differently. We have constitutionally guaranteed independent principles. But there are other principles that are extremely important. All of this results in the need for simpler and faster procedures for the revision of the non-essential provisions of the Constitution. Unfortunately, what we actually have is a product of consensus. It is a positive step that is taking place. We finally move on to distinguish the issue of the election of the President of the Republic from the dissolution of the House. It is a very important issue, as is the question of the Members' responsibility for their functions. From there on, however, we hope that in the next Review we will be able to touch on what the place needs in order to be able to move forward. Thank you very much.

Ladies and gentlemen, as early as 2013 it has been possible to launch the process of reviewing the current constitution, after the completion of the five-year period since the previous unsuccessful effort, as the New Democracy has not succeeded in creating appropriate consensus at that time. There is now a general belief that its review is pending as a historic matter in our state history for two decades, for two main reasons: firstly, because our institutional relationship with the European Union requires the constitutional review as a historical adjustment, since our Constitution has actually been drafted by a national state, which was then integrated into a supreme European institution that has even been subjected to enlargement and deepening. Secondly, because in recent years Greece has been tested by an extraordinary economic and financial crisis, which has troubled and agitated the citizens and has effectively tested the institutions and, therefore, the control of the institutions in the light of the crisis which it has experienced is a necessary political project. A Western democracy therefore finds its most genuine and true expression, the effective protection of individual and social rights, such as the right to work, to health, to education, to social security, to pay regardless of age, to decent living for all, but also to the democratic functioning of institutions and to the protection of democracy in itself. Why? Because the economic and social crisis has resulted in political extremism and a graphicization of politics and thus a complete depreciation of politics as a public function. In view of this situation, therefore, the 2019 review legislator in Greece should have as its main concern to raise the credibility, prestigiousness and price of the political system and this is an existential order of intervention for the proper functioning of the institutions. The political system has lost its contact with society and this can have dramatic consequences for the future of societies itself. The natural area, therefore, restoring the injured policy, the injured public space, is the Constitution, that is, the fundamental law which defines and organises the effective and democratic functioning of the institutions. This is where our proposal for the revision of Article 86 of the Constitution on the responsibility of Ministers, which gave the sense, not unjustly, of unjustly penalising the ministers, always in relation to ordinary citizens and at the same time gave the impression that a protected pouch of corruption has been created for the ministers. Our proposal in Article 86 therefore abolishes the short deadline for extinguishing and introduces an interpretative provision according to which the special criminal procedure of that article falls within the offences of the Ministers in the course of their duties and not on the occasion of them. I cannot, of course, fail to observe that the majority of the New Democracy's minority party expressly refuses to add this interpretative provision to Article 86 without any obvious justification, since it is constitutionally incomprehensible to accept this provision without this interpretation, since that provision will be a receptive interpretation. All these proposals, ladies and gentlemen, which concern the political staff and the restoration of the credibility and prestige of the political system, must be seen in conjunction with the constitutional provision for the democratic completion of the parliamentary and therefore the electoral cycle, because they only guarantee the necessary modernisation and rationalisation of the political system as a whole. The President of the Republic, of course, must be elected by a genuine parliamentary majority and not by a self-interested party. The proposal of the New Democracy for its election, not even by an absolute majority of the total number of Members, but by a short-term majority, degrades the President of the Republic from a symbol of the unity of the State, society and the political system, to a party leader, if it is possible for the President of the Republic to have one-party origin and one-party support. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-18-S1-commons.u190	Ladies and gentlemen, in spite of the objections, despite the unreserved reservations or criticisms, we are doing an important and rare parliamentary and legislative fact, which is the constitutional review, which, forty-five years since the establishment of the 1975 Constitution, has been essentially taking place for the third time, given the gliding content of the 2008 Review. We therefore have the third essential constitutional revision after that in 1985 and 2001. We have to think, ladies and gentlemen, that the constitutional history of our country is entitled to be regarded as privileged. The Greek world is linked to modernist constitutions. Already since the days of the Greek Revolution, the Constitution of Kings, the Constitution of 1797, with the hundreds of twenty-four articles, was a modern constitution, which provided for a single state to resolve this misunderstanding. It did not provide for a federal state, foresaw a single state and had incorporated all the posts and hopes of the Enlightenment at that time into its content. It was a liberal constitution and notice a wording. He called the people not "principal", as modern constitutions call it, but "imperial" people, Mr Scandalidis. The people were "the emperor of the people" according to the King's Constitution, which wanted to declare the strength of the representative system, but also the responsibility of the one who chooses his representatives. Then we had the constitutions of the 19th century. We have to admit that the donor Constitution, which established the Constitutional Monarchy of 1844, for its time - and not for our time, because we must judge them by their time - was an advanced constitution, despite its conservative character, because it has prevented the 'constitutional theory' by four years, as Meternich called it in 1848, which took place in most European countries, with a general uprising, which has caused great retreats of monarchy and great proliferations of the people's right to self-determination. Greece preceded four years of constitutional or liberal "theoremia", in the definition of Meternich, which swept through the European monarchies in France, Italy, Austria, in 1848. This is something that is credited for the constitutional tradition of our country and the constitutional tradition of our country has something to do with today, because we are stepping on it. We're not walking from zero. The next century, the 20th century, was the century that tested its constitutions and tested them, for they faced various crises. It is the century that we have had the two dictatorships and we have had too many political crises. It has been demonstrated by these crises that the Constitutions survive, but they are suffocating when they face divisions and severe political crises. Because the Constitutions, to breathe, need not only their texts, but they also need a spirit that is consistent with the consensus and minimum assumption of the rules of the game by all political parties involved. The 19th century, I forgot to say, was the time when the Constitutions preceded social reality. As Professor Nikolaos Alivizatos quite rightly says, it was the time when in the constitutional text one saw newness in a society that was still forward. The 21st century in which we live and the Constitution of which we prepare is the century with many questions. It is our duty, despite our disagreements and our reservations and our criticisms in one direction or another, to prepare a Constitution adapted as far as possible to the enormous needs and enormous liquidity of the 21st century, and that is why we will be judged later. ParlaMint-GR_2019-11-18-S1-commons.u205	Ladies and gentlemen, the revision of the Constitution is one of the most important functions performed by the House. The fact that the Greek Constitution is considered one of the most stringent in Europe in its Revision makes it a huge advantage of a Member's membership. It must be understood to all of us that the process of revision itself has a strong historical and political character, as it comes to a constant effort to balance two conflicting needs: the need to adapt constitutional arrangements to a constantly changing socio-political environment and the need to maintain an irreversible regulatory core. This particularly demanding process also operates as a special mirror of quality evaluation of the country's political system. Demonstrates whether or not political parties are able to get rid of micropolitical approaches and ideological strangles in order to obtain appropriate consents that will bring about the necessary changes in the constitution's Charter. Unfortunately, ladies and gentlemen, this review process has been significantly mutilated due to the proverbiality shown by the majority of the previous parliament. SYRIZA did not take over the Revision of the Constitution as an opportunity to substantially upgrade our constitution. On the contrary, he faced it with a glance at his forthcoming election defeat. That is why his review proposals find anachronism, idealism and complete indifference to the historical and social reality of Greece. SYRIZA in the previous parliamentary term approached the proposals for the revision tabled by both the New Democracy and the other opposition parties, with narrow party and ideological criteria and without the constitutional disposition required for consent. The most characteristic example of this trend was the refusal to include Article 16 in the articles to be revised. Maintaining the state monopoly in higher education, the previous parliamentary majority deprived Greece of the opportunity to develop into an international educational centre which, by exploiting our comparative advantages in education and culture, could make a significant contribution to boosting GDP. At the same time, he turned the back on thousands of remarkable Greek scientists abroad who, through the establishment of private universities, could have a realistic chance of repatriation, while at the same time challenging the retreat of new Greek students to the universities of Europe and America. The same logic was followed by the rejection of the revision of Article 24, thereby depriving our country of the possibility of creating an effective environmental policy, which is part of the modern context and, at the same time, fully in line with the challenges we face today. Under the already shaped narrow margins, the current Revision House has an obligation to proceed to a Review which, as far as possible, will release the creative forces of the country and strengthen the quality and effectiveness of our democratic culture. The seven key points proposed by the Revision Committee are indeed curing yesterday's pathogens, they respond to today's needs and they create optimism for the future. More specifically, on a proposal from the New Democracy, the incorporation in Article 21 of the Constitution of a minimum guaranteed income system demonstrates the social sensitivity of our prolongation and the intention of the Greek state to effectively protect its weakest members. The intervention in Article 64 of the Constitution towards giving the right to a committee of inquiry and the opposition parties is a very important institutional change of long-term breath, not only because it protects the parliamentary minority, but mainly strengthens the control role of the national Parliament. A key point is the separation of Article 32 of the Constitution of the election of the President of the Republic from the dissolution of the Parliament and the proclamation of parliamentary elections. This is a change that prevents the highest rule from being elected to work and is a guarantee of political stability. Facilitating the election of the independent authorities, by amending Article 101 and by establishing a 3/5 majority instead of 4/5 required to date, is another important intervention that ensures the immediate management of the independent authorities and the effective completion of their work, including the supervision and regulation of vital and sensitive areas of political, economic and social life in general. The change in Article 96(5) in relation to the equalisation of military judges with other judges strengthens the independence of military justice, removing it from the obsolete logic of the courts. A special reference should also be made to the constitutional safeguard provided for in Article 54(4) in order to facilitate the vote of Greeks abroad so that Parliament can express the wishes of the entire Greek nation regardless of whether they are within or outside the borders of the Greek state. Finally, it is also of paramount importance to intervene in Article 62 of the Constitution by limiting the immunity of the Member only in the performance of his duties, as well as the abolition of Article 86(3) of the Constitution, the amortisation period, thus including the offences of the Ministers in the normal procedure of limitation applicable to all Greek citizens. This amendment is legally correct, as it aligns itself with a strongly shaped concept both of society and of the legal world on this matter. Ladies and gentlemen, with a milestone in 2021 and the completion of two hundred years since the beginning of the Greek Revolution, the Constitutional Review had to change more. It should mark the maturity of our democracy and the passage of the country from the time of the crisis to growth and prosperity. However, despite the narrow contexts in which it is conducted, one might argue that significant institutional changes are achieved that will lead to a better and more effective functioning of our culture in the future. Thank you very much.

Ladies and gentlemen, the 1975 Constitution was shaped in conditions in which it was still in force, I would say, the so-called Kenyan policy model, and it was also a period when the demand for democratisation and social justice was strong as a result of the struggles against dictatorship. The Constitution of 1975, under the influence of these and other actors, however, has shaped, in a general and incomplete way, a framework for economic and social policy which ensured social cohesion and environmental protection, which is why it gained social legitimacy and endured time. But three developments today show not only the limits of the Constitution, but also the perceptions and beliefs which have prevailed in our society over the past decades. The first development was, as has already been rightly recognised by many speakers from all sides of the House, the crisis, bankruptcy, the victim of which was the Constitution. Obviously, it is not the Constitution that is responsible for the serious social consequences of income, living standards, employment, etc. But these consequences, the extent they have taken, indicate the need for stronger shields for the future. Many proposals from the SYRIZA Parliamentary Group therefore serve precisely this objective, namely the stronger shield against future crises, in terms of protecting labour and labour rights, the protection of public goods such as water, energy and ensuring access to them by all citizens and other provisions of the same logic. The second development that modifies our perceptions of growth, progress, sustainability and prosperity is climate change and the multiple consequences, not only in the economy, but also in life itself. Finally, the likely consequences, which are expected to be extensive from the fourth industrial revolution, in particular from the applications of robotic and artificial intelligence at work and rights, require new approaches and regulations aimed at widening democracy and preventing a new explosion of social and regional inequalities and discrimination. These developments bring new needs to the forefront that go beyond the controversy of the past, because they form a new treaty that goes beyond both the neoliberal and the Kenyan model in its traditional form. The new requirements require that we rethink and redefine the concept and criteria of progress, growth, sustainability and prosperity. For example, it is completely anachronistic to listen to the Greek Prime Minister today and a lot of the New Democracy Members talking about growth and simply mean growth of the economy when this growth has been brought to a crisis. We need in the new circumstances by a constitution that will guarantee, not the growth we have been saying before, but sustainable, sustainable growth, prosperity for all in an environment of equality and freedom. The proposals of the SYRIZA Parliamentary Group are governed by these principles and are driven by this need and are aimed at creating a new model of sustainable and fair development. This is not only about the Constitution, but also about the policy in place, it is about the development strategy which the previous government has set up despite the difficult circumstances. The wording refers to private investment in the field of education, whether an wind park you want to make, a ship you can put on coastlines, or another investment you can make in the area of education as if it were a common industry of economic activity, where the dividing lines and the discrimination between speculative and non-profit activities are erased, between goods and public goods. Here too, there was a dialogue, if the government wished to find a regulation, where the excesses, which the bankers say are being made, or even other problems, but the immunity regime introduced by the change in the Criminal Code, will have many consequences. I must tell you that both the PASOK and the New Democracy rejected the proposal. But it is not just an income, it is the limit, the level of living, which we must guarantee. Imagine someone who gets the minimum guaranteed income, as you say, but does not have access to health services or other critical services. This will be a gift of free. Therefore, in this step you are taking, which one might say is positive, the way in which you enter it is completely restrictive and does not guarantee what we need to safeguard, that is, a level of life that corresponds to the needs of our time. It will also protect work, clearly specifying that all workers, regardless of sex or other discrimination, have the right to equal pay for work of equal value. The House will be able, in other words, to set up two committees of inquiry per parliamentary term, provided that the proposal is made by ten Members and is voted for by 2/5 of all Members, irrespective of the majority. It will facilitate the emergence of the independent authorities in accordance with Article 101a. The revised provision stipulates that if a majority of eighty-eight Members are not met, a fourth and fifth votes shall be held where the absolute and the relevant majority of the candidates are required. There will no longer be the possibilities to overthrow a government from the opposition, as happened last time with the Antonis Samaras government, in which it was not allowed to complete its work with the adverse consequences that followed. As far as accountability is concerned, our prolongation is taking precedence here. Rule 62 restricts parliamentary immunity only to the parliamentary and political duties of the Member, while Article 86 abolishes the exorbitant deadline for criminal offences of Ministers and of State Secretarys, which are now being dealt with like all citizens. Therefore, through these revised provisions, the political system is self-protected by corruption phenomena and legitimate behaviours that hurt citizens and have restored their confidence in the political world. Article 68 allows the minor opposition to provoke the setting-up of committees of inquiry. Ten Members will be able to propose a committee of inquiry and if the proposal is voted for by 2/5 of all Members, a committee of inquiry will be set up. Of course, this regulation does not only concern control, but also the more democratic operation of Parliament in general. Secondly, this provision was supported by the spread of e-government to administration and justice, the consolidation of the principles of good legislation and the codification of legislation. Thirdly, we have recommended the revision of Article 24 in order to tackle climate change by ensuring water resources and renewable energy sources to strengthen the rural economy. Finally, we were clearly in favour of the establishment of private and non-profit universities, supporting the revision of Article 16. Unfortunately, these proposals were rejected and without sufficient justification, with the aim of a single one: opposition to the opposition. That was then, depriving society and the economy of a stable basis, in order to develop freely and indiscriminately in terms of the international environment and not in the past. Is it possible, ladies and gentlemen, to plan reforms and not to give our children the opportunity to study within Greece in the scientific field of their choice? Does it make sense to support the rural development of the country, without taking into account water shortages and desertification, with which areas of our country are threatened? In any case, ladies and gentlemen, our Government is moving on to the reconstruction of the administration and is constantly bringing about legislation that guarantees the sustainable development of the country and for a state that is becoming a day more and more powerful, more modern and more reliable. The work we have produced so far gives the Greeks and Greeks hope and prescribes a future that is brighter with more optimism. Thank you.

Ladies and gentlemen, I am very honoured that our resignation, the New Democracy, and our compatriots in Elias, have claimed me to participate in the composition of this Review House. It is the highest honour for every Member to participate in this topical process of Revision of the Constitution. This is a great project. Good legislation must find its best expression in the review process and it is up to all of us to be as sensible as possible, as we consult on the Constitution, especially because the arrangements that we will adopt will be binding not only on the present, but also on the future generations. As our Constitution is among the so-called 'hard constitutions', it will take at least two more parliamentary compositions until the provisions of the Constitution can be re-examined, i.e. about ten years later. Let us remember that the 1975 Constitution, as subsequently revised, is considered to be one of the most progressive and innovative constitutions for its time. It is the first European constitution that, in accordance with Article 24, introduced provisions on the protection of the natural environment, including the policy guidelines of the World Conference in Stockholm in 1972. No other state in Europe has already raised environmental protection so early on in a constitutional check. It was then incorporated into our Constitution and the dimension of environmental sustainability, as well as other dimensions. However, the Parliament proposing, to us, that is, the Pre-Accession House, which proposed in the present parliamentary composition that which articles of the Constitution should be revised, has proved to be conservative. He did not propose to revise articles which should indeed be put forward for review so that our society and our economy can become more modern and effective, to promote goals and dreams, but also to respond to the beliefs of modern society. Thus, we are not given the opportunity today to incorporate into constitutional rules modern lessons from environmental sciences, globally accepted policy directions, which are codified in international texts as the result of top environmental and, in particular, climate conferences, but also more widely accepted beliefs in society about the need to provide an increased degree of environmental protection. Through a deep conservation of the parliament proposed, Rule 24 was not put forward for revision. Our Constitution will therefore not incorporate for many more years requirements, such as for dealing with and adapting to climate change, for the transition of our economy to a low-carbon economy, for the environmentally responsible economy. If Article 24 had been proposed for revision, it could have been a constitutional requirement today to incorporate environmental criteria into the assessment of investment made in the country by domestic and foreign funds, so that in future only projects that do not harm the environment and do not adversely affect the climate will be carried out in our country. In other words, the Constitution itself could guarantee the environmentally responsible, climate neutral and sustainable nature of investments. The obligation to decontamination of polluted natural resources could also be a constitutional requirement, with particular reference to water, and specific reference should be made to quintusia, namely the protection of biodiversity. The Constitution could in future take another step and provide for a provision to protect the health and well-being of animals, which today in the Greek legal system enjoys minimal protection, revealing the joint legislator as indifferent to their rights. In a prestigious attempt to dialogue with the future reform legislator, I am letting him dare to open up in a broader form of democracy, multi-participating eco-centric democracy, recognising even quasi-generational rights to animals and nature in general. Because the Constitution that we must adopt for the benefit of future generations must shape a beautiful and harmonious environment for our children and for all beings. I appeal, in support of my own words, to the text of the most important international parliamentary body, the Interparliamentary Union, which issued a joint communiqué in Ecuador in 2013 in Kito. In Kito, parliamentarians from all over the world, expressing the people and inspired by the global objectives of sustainability and the agenda for growth 2030, were asked by their governments to shape the right conditions to achieve the ultimate goal of each constitution, which is no more than the well-being of their citizens. This is the supreme state target that is chosen as such by a series of philosophical and political texts, from the Ancient Greek Secretariat to today. However, we cannot achieve it and maintain it, but neither can we experience it in its totality but in harmony with nature. In this enlightened direction to international texts, the future revision of the Constitution should also be initiated. The removal of both Article 24 and Article 16 of the Constitution on the ability of the private sector to contribute to higher education from the list of revised provisions constitutes a loss to the Greek society and should be treated without further delay during the next review process. And the articles that have been put forward for review are all in the right direction. The introduction of the minimum guaranteed income, the change in the procedure for the election of the President of the Republic, the limitation of the immunity of MPs, the guarantees of personal and operational independence of military judges, like regular judges, the establishment of a committee of inquiry by the parliamentary minority, and in particular the activation of the Greeks' vote abroad are important regulations. I would like to congratulate the addition to Article 54(4) of the right to exercise the political right to vote for a large number of our fellow citizens living abroad, who have been unjustified for so many years, contrary to the democratic principle and the principle of popular sovereignty which the Constitution fully demands. Energising the vote of the Greeks abroad will bring us a dynamic part of Hellenism and will only be for the benefit of our country. The heart of the proven Greeks is still beating strong for Greece. With this care we show that the heart of Greece is beating just as strong for all Greeks wherever they are, in Greece or abroad. Thank you very much.
